1	//! Producing endpoint: [`Producer`] reserves the next free
2	//! slot, the [`WriteSlot`] guard writes it in place and
3	//! commits.
4	
5	use core::marker::PhantomData;
6	use core::ops::{Deref, DerefMut};
7	use core::sync::atomic::{AtomicU32, Ordering};
8	use zerocopy::{FromBytes, IntoBytes, KnownLayout};
9	
10	use crate::{Header, USER_WORDS, check_type, slot_ptr};
11	
12	/// `reserve_slot` failed: every slot holds an
13	/// uncommitted-or-unread message.
14	#[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy, PartialEq, Eq)]
15	pub struct Full;
16	
17	/// The producing endpoint: `reserve_slot`, write in place,
18	/// `commit`.
19	pub struct Producer<'a> {
20	    /// The ring's control block.
21	    header: &'a Header,
22	    /// Base of the slot array.
23	    slots: *mut u8,
24	    /// Geometry snapshot (see [`Ring`](crate::Ring)).
25	    slot_size: u32,
26	    /// Geometry snapshot (see [`Ring`](crate::Ring)).
27	    capacity: u32,
28	    /// Slot-position mask (`capacity - 1`).
29	    mask: u32,
30	    _region: PhantomData<&'a [u8]>,
31	}
32	
33	// SAFETY: the handle owns the producer role; the shared state it
34	// touches (indices) is atomic, and slot writes are handed off
35	// with Release/Acquire ordering.
36	unsafe impl Send for Producer<'_> {}
37	
38	impl<'a> Producer<'a> {
39	    /// Build the handle from [`Ring::split`](crate::Ring::split)'s
40	    /// geometry snapshot.
41	    pub(crate) fn new(
42	        header: &'a Header,
43	        slots: *mut u8,
44	        slot_size: u32,
45	        capacity: u32,
46	        mask: u32,
47	    ) -> Self {
48	        Producer {
49	            header,
50	            slots,
51	            slot_size,
52	            capacity,
53	            mask,
54	            _region: PhantomData,
55	        }
56	    }
57	
58	    /// The header's app-owned scratch line: zeroed at init,
59	    /// never touched by the crate again.
60	    ///
61	    /// - Shared with the peer: treat contents as untrusted
62	    ///   data — store values, never addresses to dereference.
63	    /// - See the design doc's "Blocking and user words" for
64	    ///   the wakeup-protocol contract it exists to host.
65	    pub fn user(&self) -> &[AtomicU32; USER_WORDS] {
66	        &self.header.user
67	    }
68	
69	    /// Reserve the next free slot as a `&mut T`, or [`Full`].
70	    ///
71	    /// - Only one slot may be reserved at a time: the guard
72	    ///   holds the `&mut Producer` borrow, so a second
73	    ///   `reserve_slot` before the guard is dropped or
74	    ///   committed does not compile.
75	    /// - Dropping the guard without [`WriteSlot::commit`]
76	    ///   abandons the reservation (nothing published).
77	    pub fn reserve_slot<T>(&mut self) -> Result<WriteSlot<'_, T>, Full>
78	    where
79	        T: FromBytes + IntoBytes + KnownLayout,
80	    {
81	        check_type::<T>(self.slot_size);
82	        let p = self.header.producer_idx.load(Ordering::Relaxed);
83	        let c = self.header.consumer_idx.load(Ordering::Acquire);
84	        // `>=`, not `==`: a peer-corrupted consumer_idx makes
85	        // occupancy look huge — fail Full, never hand out a
86	        // slot the protocol doesn't own.
87	        if p.wrapping_sub(c) >= self.capacity {
88	            return Err(Full);
89	        }
90	        // Raw pointer, not `&mut T`: a reference field would be
91	        // argument-protected for the whole `commit(self)` call,
92	        // while commit's Release store lets the consumer read
93	        // the slot inside that window — UB (Miri-verified).
94	        // Deref mints short-lived references instead.
95	        let msg = slot_ptr(self.slots, p, self.mask, self.slot_size) as *mut T;
96	        Ok(WriteSlot {
97	            header: self.header,
98	            msg,
99	            next_idx: p.wrapping_add(1),
100	            _slot: PhantomData,
101	        })
102	    }
103	
104	    /// [`reserve_slot`](Producer::reserve_slot) with an
105	    /// injected wait policy: retry until a slot frees up or
106	    /// the policy gives up.
107	    ///
108	    /// - `on_full` is called after each failed attempt with
109	    ///   the attempt count (0-based, saturating); returning
110	    ///   `false` gives up → `Err(Full)`.
111	    /// - The wait loop is the reservation itself (the guard
112	    ///   borrows the endpoint, so retrying a `reserve_slot`
113	    ///   call could not return it): `producer_idx` is ours
114	    ///   and loaded once; only `consumer_idx` is re-read per
115	    ///   attempt, so the no-wait fast path does exactly the
116	    ///   loads `reserve_slot` does.
117	    /// - See [`policy`](crate::policy) for shipped policies
118	    ///   and the composition model.
119	    pub fn reserve_slot_with<T>(
120	        &mut self,
121	        mut on_full: impl FnMut(u32) -> bool,
122	    ) -> Result<WriteSlot<'_, T>, Full>
123	    where
124	        T: FromBytes + IntoBytes + KnownLayout,
125	    {
126	        check_type::<T>(self.slot_size);
127	        // Ours alone: no peer moves it, load once.
128	        let p = self.header.producer_idx.load(Ordering::Relaxed);
129	        let mut attempt = 0u32;
130	        loop {
131	            // Same load + `>=` occupancy check as
132	            // reserve_slot (see there for the corruption
133	            // rationale).
134	            let c = self.header.consumer_idx.load(Ordering::Acquire);
135	            if p.wrapping_sub(c) < self.capacity {
136	                break;
137	            }
138	            if !on_full(attempt) {
139	                return Err(Full);
140	            }
141	            attempt = attempt.saturating_add(1);
142	        }
143	        // Raw pointer, not `&mut T` — see reserve_slot.
144	        let msg = slot_ptr(self.slots, p, self.mask, self.slot_size) as *mut T;
145	        Ok(WriteSlot {
146	            header: self.header,
147	            msg,
148	            next_idx: p.wrapping_add(1),
149	            _slot: PhantomData,
150	        })
151	    }
152	
153	    /// [`reserve_slot`](Producer::reserve_slot), spinning
154	    /// until a slot is available —
155	    /// [`reserve_slot_with`](Producer::reserve_slot_with)
156	    /// under the never-quitting
157	    /// [`policy::spin`](crate::policy::spin), so no `Result`.
158	    pub fn reserve_slot_spin<T>(&mut self) -> WriteSlot<'_, T>
159	    where
160	        T: FromBytes + IntoBytes + KnownLayout,
161	    {
162	        match self.reserve_slot_with(crate::policy::spin) {
163	            Ok(slot) => slot,
164	            Err(Full) => unreachable!("policy::spin never gives up"),
165	        }
166	    }
167	}
168	
169	/// A reserved write slot: `DerefMut` to write the message, then
170	/// [`commit`](WriteSlot::commit).
171	pub struct WriteSlot<'p, T> {
172	    /// The ring's control block (for the commit store).
173	    header: &'p Header,
174	    /// The slot, viewed as the message type. Raw on purpose —
175	    /// see the comment in [`Producer::reserve_slot`].
176	    msg: *mut T,
177	    /// Value `producer_idx` takes on commit.
178	    next_idx: u32,
179	    /// Owns the `&'p mut` borrow of the producer.
180	    _slot: PhantomData<&'p mut T>,
181	}
182	
183	impl<T> Deref for WriteSlot<'_, T> {
184	    type Target = T;
185	    /// Read access to the in-slot message.
186	    fn deref(&self) -> &T {
187	        // SAFETY: msg is in-bounds and aligned (check_type +
188	        // cache-line slot base), any byte pattern is a valid T
189	        // (FromBytes bound at reserve_slot), and the index
190	        // protocol gives this guard exclusive slot access until
191	        // commit.
192	        unsafe { &*self.msg }
193	    }
194	}
195	
196	impl<T> DerefMut for WriteSlot<'_, T> {
197	    /// Write access to the in-slot message.
198	    fn deref_mut(&mut self) -> &mut T {
199	        // SAFETY: as in deref; &mut self gives exclusivity of
200	        // the minted reference.
201	        unsafe { &mut *self.msg }
202	    }
203	}
204	
205	impl<T> WriteSlot<'_, T> {
206	    /// Publish the slot to the consumer
207	    /// (`producer_idx + 1`, `Release`).
208	    pub fn commit(self) {
209	        self.header
210	            .producer_idx
211	            .store(self.next_idx, Ordering::Release);
212	    }
213	}
214	
```

1	//! Consuming endpoint: [`Consumer`] reserves the oldest unread
2	//! slot, the [`ReadSlot`] guard reads it in place and releases.
3	
4	use core::marker::PhantomData;
5	use core::ops::Deref;
6	use core::sync::atomic::{AtomicU32, Ordering};
7	use zerocopy::{FromBytes, Immutable, KnownLayout};
8	
9	use crate::{Header, USER_WORDS, check_type, slot_ptr};
10	
11	/// `reserve_slot` failed: no unread messages.
12	#[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy, PartialEq, Eq)]
13	pub struct Empty;
14	
15	/// The consuming endpoint: `reserve_slot` the oldest unread
16	/// slot, read in place, `release`.
17	pub struct Consumer<'a> {
18	    /// The ring's control block.
19	    header: &'a Header,
20	    /// Base of the slot array.
21	    slots: *mut u8,
22	    /// Geometry snapshot (see [`Ring`](crate::Ring)).
23	    slot_size: u32,
24	    /// Slot-position mask (`capacity - 1`).
25	    mask: u32,
26	    _region: PhantomData<&'a [u8]>,
27	}
28	
29	// SAFETY: the handle owns the consumer role; see the Producer
30	// Send rationale.
31	unsafe impl Send for Consumer<'_> {}
32	
33	impl<'a> Consumer<'a> {
34	    /// Build the handle from [`Ring::split`](crate::Ring::split)'s
35	    /// geometry snapshot.
36	    pub(crate) fn new(header: &'a Header, slots: *mut u8, slot_size: u32, mask: u32) -> Self {
37	        Consumer {
38	            header,
39	            slots,
40	            slot_size,
41	            mask,
42	            _region: PhantomData,
43	        }
44	    }
45	
46	    /// The header's app-owned scratch line: zeroed at init,
47	    /// never touched by the crate again.
48	    ///
49	    /// - Shared with the peer: treat contents as untrusted
50	    ///   data — store values, never addresses to dereference.
51	    /// - See the design doc's "Blocking and user words" for
52	    ///   the wakeup-protocol contract it exists to host.
53	    pub fn user(&self) -> &[AtomicU32; USER_WORDS] {
54	        &self.header.user
55	    }
56	
57	    /// Reserve the oldest unread slot as a `&T`, or [`Empty`].
58	    ///
59	    /// - Only one slot may be reserved at a time: the guard
60	    ///   holds the `&mut Consumer` borrow, so a second
61	    ///   `reserve_slot` before the guard is dropped or released
62	    ///   does not compile.
63	    /// - Dropping the guard without [`ReadSlot::release`]
64	    ///   leaves the slot unread — the next `reserve_slot`
65	    ///   returns it again.
66	    pub fn reserve_slot<T>(&mut self) -> Result<ReadSlot<'_, T>, Empty>
67	    where
68	        T: FromBytes + KnownLayout + Immutable,
69	    {
70	        check_type::<T>(self.slot_size);
71	        let c = self.header.consumer_idx.load(Ordering::Relaxed);
72	        let p = self.header.producer_idx.load(Ordering::Acquire);
73	        if p == c {
74	            return Err(Empty);
75	        }
76	        // Raw pointer, not `&T`, for the same reason as in the
77	        // producer's reserve_slot: release's store frees the
78	        // slot for producer writes while a reference field
79	        // would still be argument-protected.
80	        let msg = slot_ptr(self.slots, c, self.mask, self.slot_size) as *const T;
81	        Ok(ReadSlot {
82	            header: self.header,
83	            msg,
84	            next_idx: c.wrapping_add(1),
85	            _slot: PhantomData,
86	        })
87	    }
88	
89	    /// [`reserve_slot`](Consumer::reserve_slot) with an
90	    /// injected wait policy: retry until a message arrives or
91	    /// the policy gives up.
92	    ///
93	    /// - `on_empty` is called after each failed attempt with
94	    ///   the attempt count (0-based, saturating); returning
95	    ///   `false` gives up → `Err(Empty)`.
96	    /// - The wait loop is the reservation itself (the guard
97	    ///   borrows the endpoint, so retrying a `reserve_slot`
98	    ///   call could not return it): `consumer_idx` is ours
99	    ///   and loaded once; only `producer_idx` is re-read per
100	    ///   attempt, so the no-wait fast path does exactly the
101	    ///   loads `reserve_slot` does.
102	    /// - See [`policy`](crate::policy) for shipped policies
103	    ///   and the composition model.
104	    pub fn reserve_slot_with<T>(
105	        &mut self,
106	        mut on_empty: impl FnMut(u32) -> bool,
107	    ) -> Result<ReadSlot<'_, T>, Empty>
108	    where
109	        T: FromBytes + KnownLayout + Immutable,
110	    {
111	        check_type::<T>(self.slot_size);
112	        // Ours alone: no peer moves it, load once.
113	        let c = self.header.consumer_idx.load(Ordering::Relaxed);
114	        let mut attempt = 0u32;
115	        loop {
116	            let p = self.header.producer_idx.load(Ordering::Acquire);
117	            if p != c {
118	                break;
119	            }
120	            if !on_empty(attempt) {
121	                return Err(Empty);
122	            }
123	            attempt = attempt.saturating_add(1);
124	        }
125	        // Raw pointer, not `&T` — see reserve_slot.
126	        let msg = slot_ptr(self.slots, c, self.mask, self.slot_size) as *const T;
127	        Ok(ReadSlot {
128	            header: self.header,
129	            msg,
130	            next_idx: c.wrapping_add(1),
131	            _slot: PhantomData,
132	        })
133	    }
134	
135	    /// [`reserve_slot`](Consumer::reserve_slot), spinning
136	    /// until a message arrives —
137	    /// [`reserve_slot_with`](Consumer::reserve_slot_with)
138	    /// under the never-quitting
139	    /// [`policy::spin`](crate::policy::spin), so no `Result`.
140	    pub fn reserve_slot_spin<T>(&mut self) -> ReadSlot<'_, T>
141	    where
142	        T: FromBytes + KnownLayout + Immutable,
143	    {
144	        match self.reserve_slot_with(crate::policy::spin) {
145	            Ok(slot) => slot,
146	            Err(Empty) => unreachable!("policy::spin never gives up"),
147	        }
148	    }
149	}
150	
151	/// A reserved read slot: `Deref` to read the message, then
152	/// [`release`](ReadSlot::release).
153	pub struct ReadSlot<'c, T> {
154	    /// The ring's control block (for the release store).
155	    header: &'c Header,
156	    /// The slot, viewed as the message type. Raw on purpose —
157	    /// see the comment in [`Consumer::reserve_slot`].
158	    msg: *const T,
159	    /// Value `consumer_idx` takes on release.
160	    next_idx: u32,
161	    /// Owns the `&'c` borrow of the consumer.
162	    _slot: PhantomData<&'c T>,
163	}
164	
165	impl<T> Deref for ReadSlot<'_, T> {
166	    type Target = T;
167	    /// Read access to the in-slot message.
168	    fn deref(&self) -> &T {
169	        // SAFETY: msg is in-bounds and aligned (check_type +
170	        // cache-line slot base), any byte pattern is a valid T
171	        // (FromBytes bound at reserve_slot), and the index
172	        // protocol gives this guard read access until release.
173	        unsafe { &*self.msg }
174	    }
175	}
176	
177	impl<T> ReadSlot<'_, T> {
178	    /// Free the slot for reuse (`consumer_idx + 1`, `Release`).
179	    pub fn release(self) {
180	        self.header
181	            .consumer_idx
182	            .store(self.next_idx, Ordering::Release);
183	    }
184	}
185	
```

1	//! Two-threaded `std::sync::mpsc` round-trip bench, spin waits.
2	
3	use std::hint::black_box;
4	use std::sync::mpsc::{self, TryRecvError};
5	use std::thread;
6	
7	use crate::harness::{self, Bench, RunCfg};
8	use crate::pin;
9	
10	/// Registry name used on the CLI.
11	pub const NAME: &str = "mpsc-2t-spin";
12	
13	/// Main → worker → main round-trip over two `std::sync::mpsc`
14	/// channels where both ends spin on `try_recv` instead of parking
15	/// in `recv`. Same wait policy as the `ice-*-2t` benches
16	/// (non-blocking receive + `spin_loop`), so `mpsc-2t-spin` vs
17	/// `ice-ps-2t` is a pure transport comparison — in-process channel
18	/// queue vs shared-memory queue — while `mpsc-2t` vs this bench
19	/// isolates the park/wake cost of blocking `recv`.
20	pub struct StdMpsc2ThreadSpin {
21	    req_tx: mpsc::Sender<u64>,
22	    resp_rx: mpsc::Receiver<u64>,
23	    worker: Option<thread::JoinHandle<()>>,
24	    counter: u64,
25	}
26	
27	impl StdMpsc2ThreadSpin {
28	    /// Spawn the spinning echo worker, optionally pinning it to
29	    /// `worker_cpu`.
30	    pub fn new(worker_cpu: Option<usize>) -> Self {
31	        let (req_tx, req_rx) = mpsc::channel::<u64>();
32	        let (resp_tx, resp_rx) = mpsc::channel::<u64>();
33	        let worker = thread::spawn(move || {
34	            pin::pin_current(worker_cpu);
35	            loop {
36	                match req_rx.try_recv() {
37	                    Ok(v) => {
38	                        if resp_tx.send(v).is_err() {
39	                            break;
40	                        }
41	                    }
42	                    Err(TryRecvError::Empty) => core::hint::spin_loop(),
43	                    Err(TryRecvError::Disconnected) => break,
44	                }
45	            }
46	        });
47	        Self {
48	            req_tx,
49	            resp_rx,
50	            worker: Some(worker),
51	            counter: 0,
52	        }
53	    }
54	}
55	
56	impl Bench for StdMpsc2ThreadSpin {
57	    fn name(&self) -> &str {
58	        "mpsc-2t-spin: std::sync::mpsc round-trip (2 threads, spin)"
59	    }
60	
61	    fn step(&mut self) -> u64 {
62	        self.counter = self.counter.wrapping_add(1);
63	        self.req_tx.send(self.counter).unwrap();
64	        loop {
65	            match self.resp_rx.try_recv() {
66	                Ok(v) => return black_box(v),
67	                Err(TryRecvError::Empty) => core::hint::spin_loop(),
68	                Err(TryRecvError::Disconnected) => panic!("worker gone"),
69	            }
70	        }
71	    }
72	}
73	
74	impl Drop for StdMpsc2ThreadSpin {
75	    fn drop(&mut self) {
76	        // Replace req_tx with a dummy so we can drop the real one;
77	        // worker's try_recv() then returns Disconnected and the
78	        // worker exits. Same shape as mpsc-2t's Drop.
79	        let (dummy_tx, _) = mpsc::channel();
80	        drop(std::mem::replace(&mut self.req_tx, dummy_tx));
81	        if let Some(worker) = self.worker.take() {
82	            let _ = worker.join();
83	        }
84	    }
85	}
86	
87	/// Registry entry point.
88	pub fn run(cfg: &RunCfg) {
89	    let mut bench = StdMpsc2ThreadSpin::new(cfg.core_for(1));
90	    let (hist, outer, inner, duration_s) = harness::run_adaptive(&mut bench, cfg);
91	    harness::print_report(bench.name(), outer, inner, duration_s, &hist, cfg.overhead);
92	}
93	
```

38:    (ice_rr_2t::NAME, ice_rr_2t::run),
39:];
40:
41:/// All registered bench names, in [`REGISTRY`] order. Used for CLI
42:/// help and the `all` resolution.
43:pub fn names() -> Vec<&'static str> {
44:    REGISTRY.iter().map(|(n, _)| *n).collect()
45:}
46:
47:/// Resolve a list of CLI-requested names (or the literal `"all"`)
48:/// to an ordered list of [`RunFn`]s. A name that matches no bench
49:/// exactly runs every bench it is a prefix of (`ice` → all four
50:/// ice benches, `mpsc` → both mpsc benches), in [`REGISTRY`]
51:/// order. Returns an error on any name matching nothing.
52:pub fn resolve(requested: &[String]) -> Result<Vec<RunFn>, String> {
53:    if requested.iter().any(|n| n == "all") {
54:        return Ok(REGISTRY.iter().map(|(_, run)| *run).collect());
55:    }
56:
57:    let mut runners = Vec::with_capacity(requested.len());
58:    for name in requested {
59:        if let Some((_, run)) = REGISTRY.iter().find(|(n, _)| n == name) {
60:            runners.push(*run);
61:            continue;
62:        }
63:        let prefixed: Vec<RunFn> = REGISTRY
64:            .iter()
65:            .filter(|(n, _)| n.starts_with(name.as_str()))
66:            .map(|(_, run)| *run)
67:            .collect();
68:        if prefixed.is_empty() {
69:            return Err(format!(
70:                "unknown bench '{name}'. valid: all, {}",
71:                self::names().join(", ")
72:            ));
73:        }
74:        runners.extend(prefixed);
75:    }
76:    Ok(runners)
77:}

> AGENT

I've reacquainted. Here's the picture and my thoughts.

**What zc-ring-x1 offers** (v0.7.2, `../zc-ring-x1`): SPSC ring with three API tiers on each endpoint —

1. **Raw**: `reserve_slot::<T>() -> Result<WriteSlot/ReadSlot, Full/Empty>` — caller handles the retry loop.
2. **Closure**: `reserve_slot_with::<T>(on_full/on_empty)` — the wait loop is inside the reservation; the closure decides retry/give-up per attempt.
3. **Easy**: `reserve_slot_spin::<T>()` — `reserve_slot_with(policy::spin)`, no `Result`.

The docs on `reserve_slot_with` already claim the no-wait fast path does exactly the same loads as `reserve_slot`, so the hypothesis "all three perform basically the same" is a doc claim worth verifying — a nice fit for this repo.

**Bench design thoughts:**

- **Shape**: mirror the existing round-trip pattern — a 1t variant (same-thread ping-pong through two rings, or one ring send-then-receive) and a 2t variant (echo worker, spin waits, pinned via `cfg.core_for(1)`), like `mpsc-2t-spin` and the `ice-*` benches. Two rings (req + resp), `u64` payload — `u64` already satisfies the zerocopy bounds, so no custom `Msg` type needed, and it matches the counter-echo shape of the other benches.
- **The 1t variant is the cleanest tier comparison**: no contention, the ring is never full/empty on the hot path, so any delta between tiers is pure API overhead. The 2t variant adds the real cross-core story and compares against `mpsc-2t-spin`/`ice-ps-2t` at the same wait policy.
- **Raw-tier wait policy in 2t**: hand-written `loop { match reserve_slot() { Ok=>.., Err=>spin_loop() } }` — that's the natural "user does it themselves" baseline the closure/spin tiers get compared to.
- **Lifetime wrinkle**: `Ring<'a>` borrows the region, so a bench struct holding a `Producer` plus a spawned worker is self-referential. Simplest fix: `Box::leak` the two regions so endpoints are `'static` (a bench process leaking 2×~few-KiB is fine; can note it).
- **Naming**: `zcr-1t` / `zcr-2t` (raw), then `zcr-with-1t/2t` and `zcr-spin-1t/2t` later — prefix `zcr` runs the whole family. Open to `zcr-raw-*` instead if you'd rather all six be explicit.
- **Dependency**: path dep `zc-ring-x1 = { path = "../zc-ring-x1" }`. Worth noting in the todo that this makes the repo build depend on a sibling checkout.

**Versioning proposal** (needs your call per CLAUDE.md): multi-step `0.12.0` —
- `dev1`: plan note in chores
- `dev2`: dep + raw benches `zcr-1t` / `zcr-2t`
- `dev3`: `zcr-with-*`
- `dev4`: `zcr-spin-*` + comparison run/table
- `0.12.0` release

Sound good? Two things to confirm: naming (`zcr-1t` vs `zcr-raw-1t`) and multi-step vs single-step.

```
1	# AGENTS.md - Bot Instructions
2	
3	## Project Structure
4	
5	This project uses **two separate jj-git repos**:
6	
7	1. **App repo** (`/` — project root): the project's generated
8	   artifact — code, prose, image, song, whatever it produces.
9	2. **Bot session repo** (`/.claude/`): Contains Claude Code session data.
10	
11	Both repos are managed with `jj` (Jujutsu), which coexists with git.
12	
13	**Committing vs pushing.** Default to `vc-x1 push` — it commits and
14	publishes both repos together, each carrying one `ochid:` pointing at
15	the other (see [Cycle Protocol](#cycle-protocol) and
16	[ochid trailers](#cross-repo-linking-ochid-trailers)). Use a bare
17	`jj commit` (see [jj Basics](#jj-basics)) only when:
18	
19	- the change will never be published — no `ochid:`;
20	- the commit will be squashed away before its series is pushed
21	  (loop-and-squash) — no `ochid:`; it disappears in the squash;
22	- the commit will be pushed later as a non-top commit — `jj commit`
23	  and add its `ochid:` now, because `vc-x1 push` stamps only the
24	  topmost commit it pushes, never the ancestors.
25	
26	**What "commit" and "push" mean.** In an instruction, "commit",
27	"push", and "commit + push" all mean `vc-x1 push` — land *and*
28	publish — unless stated otherwise. A bare `jj commit` is asked for
29	by name: "local commit", "save locally", "just `jj commit`" (the
30	cases above). So the spoken default is publish; the local-only
31	save is the named exception. The approval around a push —
32	interactive by default, waived only by an explicit scoped
33	delegation — is the cycle protocol's
34	[Pushing policy](notes/cycle-protocol.md#policy).
35	
36	## Repo Paths (relative from project root)
37	
38	- App repo: `.` (project root)
39	- Bot session repo: `.claude`
40	  (symlink from `~/.claude/projects/<path-to-project-root>/.claude`)
41	
42	## Working Directory
43	
44	Prefer staying in the project root. Use `-R` flags or absolute paths
45	to target other directories rather than `cd`. If `cd` seems necessary,
46	discuss with the user first — losing track of cwd causes subtle
47	command failures downstream.
48	
49	When invoking shell commands, prefer the shortest unambiguous path —
50	usually relative to cwd (`ls notes/`, not
51	`ls /home/wink/data/prgs/rust/vc-x1/notes/`). Long absolute paths
52	clutter the transcript without adding information. Out-of-workspace
53	paths (`/tmp/...`, `~/.config/...`) stay absolute. Read/Edit/Write
54	tool args also stay absolute — that's a tool-boundary constraint,
55	not a stylistic choice.
56	
57	Run **one command per shell invocation** — don't bundle several
58	steps into a single compound script (`a && b; c`). Bundling hides
59	which step produced which output and makes a failure ambiguous;
60	executing one at a time keeps the details visible and reviewable.
61	Exceptions are a genuine pipeline (`grep | sort`) or a tight,
62	inseparable pair where the join is the point.
63	
64	## File reads — read the slice you need
65	
66	Long notes files are appended to over time. Read only the
67	slice your task needs; grep or read further on demand.
68	
69	- **`notes/todo.md`** (the routine acquaint read) — first
70	  ~60 lines covers intro + `## In Progress` + the top of the
71	  ranked `## Todo` (priorities, #1 highest). `Read` with
72	  `offset=0, limit=60`. `## Ideas` sits below `## Todo`; read
73	  further only when chasing a lower-ranked entry, an Idea, a
74	  `[N]` ref, or auditing the whole list.
75	- **`notes/todo-backlog.md`** — the long-tail backlog
76	  (lower-priority entries below the ranked `## Todo`). Read
77	  only when picking up a backlog item; grep to locate it
78	  first.
79	- **`notes/bugs.md`** — the bug list. Small; read whole
80	  when triaging a bug or chasing the `## Bugs` pointer in
81	  todo.md.
82	- **`notes/done.md`** + **`notes/chores/chores-NN.md`** —
83	  historical / append-mostly. Scan headings first
84	  (`grep '^## ' notes/chores/chores-NN.md`), then read only
85	  the section you need.
86	
87	**Why:** the routine read should stay small. `notes/todo.md`
88	grows every cycle, so the backlog and bugs live in sibling
89	files rather than inline; the same "slice you need" rule
90	applies to historical files.
91	
92	## Memory
93	
94	Do not use the bot's per-project memory directory
95	(`~/.claude/projects/<path>/memory/`). In a dual-repo setup with
96	AGENTS.md it provides no capability AGENTS.md doesn't already cover,
97	and it loses on discoverability:
98	
99	- **AGENTS.md** — at the repo root, a well-known location for bot
100	  instructions, committed, reviewable, visible to every collaborator
101	  (human or bot).
102	- **Memory directory** — hidden under the user's home, tied to one
103	  machine, invisible to anyone but the bot, never diffed or reviewed.
104	
105	Easy for everyone to find beats convenient for the bot alone. Put
106	durable context in AGENTS.md (or committed `notes/`) instead.
107	
108	## Speculation marker
109	
110	Durable text the bot writes — AGENTS.md, `notes/`, commit bodies,
111	chores sections — should stick to observations and direct descriptions
112	of the code or data. If a mechanism, hypothesis, or causal claim
113	enters the text, prefix it with "We think ..." (a royal "we") so a
114	reader can tell the measured from the inferred.
115	
116	**Why:** unmarked speculation reads like evidence, and a future reader
117	(or the bot on a later session) can pick it up as a known fact when
118	it's not. Measured / inferred is a distinction worth keeping visible
119	in the written record.
120	
121	**How to apply:** observations and factual descriptions need no
122	marker. Prefix with "We think ..." (or a close variant like
123	"Our guess is ...") when the claim is a mechanism ("X wins
124	because Y caches better"), a cause ("the drift was due to thermal
125	state"), a prediction ("this should scale linearly"), or any
126	reasoning not directly supported by the data on hand.
127	
128	## jj Basics
129	
130	**Use jj, not git, for version-control operations** (status, log,
131	diff, commit, push, history rewrite). jj coexists with the git
132	backend, so the repo *can* be driven with raw `git`, but this
133	project's workflow — bookmarks, the working-copy `@` model, ochid
134	trailers — is expressed in jj terms; reaching for `git` invites
135	state that doesn't match the jj documentation here. There is no
136	`jj mv`: to move/rename a tracked file, just `mv` it on disk and
137	jj detects the rename by content.
138	
139	- `jj st -R .` / `jj st -R .claude` — show working copy status
140	- `jj log -R .` / `jj log -R .claude` — show commit log
141	- `jj commit -m "title" -m "body" -R <repo>` — finalize working copy into a commit
142	- `jj describe -m "title" -m "body" -R <repo>` — set description without committing
143	- `jj git push --bookmark <name> -R <repo>` — push a bookmark (no
144	  `--allow-new` flag; jj pushes new bookmarks without special flags)
145	- In jj, the working copy (@) is always a mutable commit being edited.
146	  `jj commit` finalizes it and creates a new empty working copy on top.
147	- The `.claude` repo always has uncommitted changes during an active
148	  session because session data updates continuously.
149	- `jj rebase` uses `--onto`/`-o` to name the destination(s).
150	
151	## Cross-repo linking (ochid trailers)
152	
153	The cross-reference between the app repo and the `.claude` bot
154	repo is what makes the dual-repo work: every commit points at its
155	counterpart in the other repo, so the "what" (code) and the
156	"why / how" (session) stay linked across time. That pointer is the
157	**ochid** (Other Change ID) git trailer.
158	
159	A **chid** is jj's change ID — a permanent identifier that
160	survives rebases and `describe`s (unlike the commit ID / git SHA,
161	which changes on rewrite). An **ochid** trailer carries the
162	counterpart commit's chid as a workspace-root-relative path:
163	
164	- Paths start with `/` — the workspace root, i.e. the app repo
165	  (vc-template-x1). `/.claude` is the bot session sub-repo.
166	- `ochid: /<chid>` references a change in the **app repo**.
167	- `ochid: /.claude/<chid>` references a change in the **`.claude`
168	  repo**.
169	
170	Trailers are blank-line-separated `key: value` lines at the end of
171	the commit body, using the chid's **12-character** prefix:
172	
173	```
174	ochid: /.claude/xvzvruqowktp   # points to a .claude repo change
175	ochid: /wtpmottvxqzl           # points to an app repo change
176	```
177	
178	How many, and which direction:
179	
180	- **Code-side commits** each carry one
181	  `ochid: /.claude/<.claude-chid>` — the `.claude` change ID.
182	- **The `.claude` commit** carries one `ochid: /<code-chid>` per
183	  code commit in that push. More than one occurs on a Merge
184	  non-ff close-out (one ochid per Work commit in the cycle).
185	
186	Use `vc-x1 chid -s code,bot -L` to capture the change IDs (first
187	line app, second `.claude`).
188	
189	### Resolvability
190	
191	A change ID travels with its commit: a **pushed** commit resolves
192	to the same chid in every clone — cloning the `.claude` repo gave
193	the published `main` tip the same chid as an existing clone. We
194	think jj carries the change ID in the git commit object, so it
195	survives `jj git clone` / fetch.
196	
197	The local-only case is the **working-copy `@`**: jj mints a fresh
198	random chid for `@` in each clone, so an unpushed `@` is never a
199	stable ochid target. This is why a `.claude` ochid names `@-`
200	(the last committed change), not `@`.
201	
202	### .vc-config.toml
203	
204	Each repo contains a `.vc-config.toml` that identifies its
205	location within the workspace, so tools resolve these paths
206	without repeating the workspace path in every trailer:
207	
208	```toml
209	# In vc-template-x1 (workspace root):
210	[workspace]
211	path = "/"
212	
213	# In .claude (sub-repo):
214	[workspace]
215	path = "/.claude"
216	```
217	
218	## Prose form
219	
220	Long-lived prose on this project follows one basic shape: a short intro
221	that explains the *why* or the high-level *what*, then a `-` bullet
222	list for the details. Wrap lines at ≤72 cols (bullet continuations
223	indent two spaces). Avoid wall-of-prose paragraphs — they hide the
224	structure that bullets make scannable.
225	
226	Surfaces that use this shape:
227	
228	- Module / function / struct / field doc comments in `.rs` files —
229	  see [Doc comments](#doc-comments-on-every-file-function-and-method).
230	- Commit message bodies (both app-repo and session-repo). The
231	  ≤50-col title is the commit-specific add-on; see
232	  [Per-commit flow](notes/cycle-protocol.md#per-commit-flow).
233	- Chore descriptions in `notes/chores/chores-NN.md` — see
234	  [Chores section content](#chores-section-content--no-edit-list-git-is-the-record).
235	- Todo and Done entries in `notes/todo.md` when an entry needs more
236	  than one line of detail. Pure one-liners are still fine.
237	
238	Bullet *content* differs by surface:
239	
240	- **Commit bodies** — bullets are file-by-file: one bullet per file
241	  changed, file plus a one-line gist (e.g.
242	  `README.md: new Overview intro`). Source of truth for the
243	  mechanical edit list.
244	- **Chores / todo / done** — bullets are conceptual (design points,
245	  structural notes, the "what landed and why" at a notch above
246	  file-list granularity). Never a copy of the commit's edit list —
247	  see [Chores section content](#chores-section-content--no-edit-list-git-is-the-record).
248	- **Doc comments** — bullets are whatever structure fits (fields,
249	  cases, invariants).
250	
251	### Problem + plan shape
252	
253	`## In Progress` cycle blocks, chores section intros, and `## Todo`
254	entries use a sharper form of the same shape:
255	
256	- **Problem statement** (the why) — one or two sentences;
257	  don't pad with intent, don't restate the plan.
258	- **Plan bullets** (the what/when) — formality differs by
259	  surface:
260	  - In Progress / chores: a committed ladder, one step per
261	    commit — see
262	    [Conventional-commit shape](#conventional-commit-shape-ladder--chores--commit)
263	    for the per-step title + `(current)` / `(done)` form.
264	  - Todo entries: rough informal bullets, no numbering;
265	    formalized only when the entry is picked up into a
266	    cycle.
267	
268	### Semicolons inside bullets
269	
270	A bullet that joins multiple clauses with semicolons (`A; B; C`)
271	is a list hiding inside running prose — break the clauses into
272	sub-bullets so the structure shows. Semicolons in running prose
273	(intro paragraphs, sentence-joins) are fine. Not absolute: very
274	short clauses or tight pairs can stay joined inside a bullet when
275	breaking would be more noise than signal.
276	
277	### Conventional-commit shape (ladder / chores / commit)
278	
279	A ladder step, its chores section, and its commit description
280	share a *title* shape — a
281	[Conventional Commits](https://www.conventionalcommits.org/en/v1.0.0/)
282	title over [Prose form](#prose-form) detail. They differ in the
283	title's prefix / marker (below) and in bullet *content* — commit
284	bodies are file-by-file, ladder / chores conceptual (see "Bullet
285	*content* differs by surface" above). The shared template:
286	
287	```
288	<optional - X.Y.Z[-N]> <title>   # <title> is the commit's `<type>: <desc>`
289	<optional prose intro>
290	  - <optional item>
291	    <optional prose intro>
292	      - <optional sub-item>
293	      ...
294	```
295	
296	The three surfaces apply it as:
297	
298	- **Ladder step** (`notes/todo.md` `## In Progress`): the title is
299	  prefixed with the version — `X.Y.Z-N <title>` — and
300	  carries a `(current)` / `(done)` marker. The bare three-element
301	  `X.Y.Z` (no `-N`) is the close-out step. Detail is bulleted,
302	  never `;`-joined inline.
303	- **Chores section** (`notes/chores/chores-NN.md`): no version
304	  prefix — the `##` header *is* the bare title, with a
305	  `Commits: [[ref]]` line first under it (empty until backfilled —
306	  see [Chores commit references](#chores-commit-references)).
307	- **Commit description**: no version prefix — the title is the
308	  ≤50-col first line; the body is the prose (file-by-file for the
309	  app repo, per [Per-commit flow](notes/cycle-protocol.md#per-commit-flow)).
310	
311	The title is **identical** across all three for a given step, so a
312	step's ladder entry, its chores `##` header, and its commit title
313	line up verbatim — pick the commit title first and reuse it.
314	
315	That identity is **per step**, not per cycle: each step in a
316	cycle gets its own distinct descriptive title — never one shared
317	cycle title uniquified by a step marker. The cycle's chores
318	section header carries the anticipated *close-out* title. To keep
319	a cycle's commits collectable with one `git log --grep`, give the
320	step titles a common greppable stem (e.g. `config loader`).
321	
322	## Notes file conventions
323	
324	Conventions the bot follows when writing notes files
325	(`notes/todo.md`, `notes/todo-backlog.md`, `notes/bugs.md`,
326	`notes/chores/chores-NN.md`, `notes/done.md`). One source
327	of truth lives here; [`notes/README.md`](notes/README.md)
328	points back.
329	
330	### Notes references
331	
332	Reference *citations* are double-bracketed so the brackets render
333	— `[[N]]`, or `[[2]],[[3]]` for several (comma-separated, not
334	`[2,3]` or `[[2]][[3]]`). The `[N]:` definitions in a file's
335	`# References` section and inline `[text](url)` / `[text](#anchor)`
336	links stay single-bracketed.
337	
338	### Reference numbering
339	
340	Every note file (`todo.md`, `todo-backlog.md`, `bugs.md`,
341	`chores-NN.md`, `done.md`) keeps a file-local `# References`
342	section at the bottom. Reference numbers are scoped to that
343	file — `[1]` in `chores-07.md` and `[1]` in `chores-01.md`
344	are independent slots that may point at completely different
345	URLs. New chores files start their numbering at `[1]`.
346	
347	Treat `[N]` like a **footnote**: the number is a local slot, only
348	meaningful within its file's `# References`. So a `[N]` *citation*
349	(bare `[N]`, or doubled `[[N]]`) never reuses another file's number; to cite a target a sibling
350	file references, pick your own next-local slot and define it
351	(the same target may carry a different number in each file). A
352	`[N]` *inside a code span* (`` `[72]` ``) is different — that's
353	a quoted identifier: literal text naming a ref-key (often from
354	another file's namespace), data, not a citation; it needs no
355	definition here. To point at a section of another file from
356	prose, use an inline link with an anchor —
357	`[that section](../chores-07.md#…)` — not a bare number.
358	
359	A `chores-NN.md` `# References` entry is usually a
360	`/notes/<file>.md#anchor` (or `/ARCHITECTURE.md`) path, but may
361	also be a **commit reference** —
362	`[N]: <commit-url-with-12-hex-SHA> "<full-40-hex-SHA>"`, cited by
363	a section's `Commits:` line. See
364	[Chores commit references](#chores-commit-references) for the
365	why and the exact shape.
366	
367	A file's `# References` can be **re-packed** to a contiguous
368	`[1]..[N]` in first-citation-appearance order — walk the file's
369	prose in document order (`todo.md`: `## Todo` then `## Done`;
370	`chores-NN.md`: top to bottom) and number refs as their first
371	`[[N]]` citation appears. This is a file-local rewrite — only
372	that file's `[[N]]` citations and `[N]:` definitions move; every
373	target and sibling file is untouched. A `[[N]]` inside a `` ` ``
374	code span is a literal token, not a citation, and is left alone.
375	Do it opportunistically (when the namespace has drifted enough to
376	annoy), not on a schedule: `todo.md` fragments fastest (entries
377	land and get pruned every cycle) and is the usual candidate;
378	`chores-NN.md` / `done.md` are append-mostly and only need it
379	after an unusual event (e.g. a bulk retrofit that allocated slots
380	out of document order).
381	
382	### Markdown anchor links
383	
384	GitHub anchor algorithm: lowercase, strip non-alphanumeric
385	characters in place, map remaining spaces to hyphens 1-for-1. Do
386	**not** collapse adjacent whitespace — so `a + b` → `a--b` (spaces
387	on both sides of `+`), but `a: b` → `a-b` (only trailing space on
388	`:`). General markdown reference:
389	[markdownguide.org](https://www.markdownguide.org). GitHub
390	publishes no official spec for auto-generated anchors; the
391	de-facto reference implementation is
392	[github-slugger](https://github.com/Flet/github-slugger).
393	
394	### Todo format
395	
396	`todo.md` is organized into `## In Progress`, `## Todo`
397	(strict priority rank, #1 highest; long-tail backlog in
398	[todo-backlog.md](notes/todo-backlog.md)), `## Ideas`,
399	`## Bugs` (pointer to [bugs.md](notes/bugs.md)), and
400	`## Done` sections. Each item is a short description with
401	reference links to more detail.
402	
403	`## Todo` and `## Bugs` entries carry explicit `1.` `2.` …
404	numbers in the source. For `## Todo` the number is its
405	**priority rank** (#1 highest, descending); for `## Bugs`
406	it's just an index. They're for grepping and at-a-glance
407	"let's do #1" — **not stable IDs**: reorder (to
408	reprioritize), insert, or delete freely, then
409	`vc-x1 fix-todo --no-dry-run` renumbers and normalizes
410	continuation-line indent, so any given number is positional.
411	**To refer to a Todo durably, name it by its title — a
412	plain, greppable text mention.** Not its number (positional,
413	renumbered) and not a markdown link: a numbered list item has
414	no anchor to link to.
415	
416	Numbering helps a human orient in a long list but makes links
417	difficult and fragile — especially an external reference
418	pointing in, which can't be auto-fixed when the list
419	renumbers. A robust fix (a number-free anchor, or a
420	number-tolerant dereference that matches the title slug and
421	wildcards the numeric prefix — a GitHub slug like `5-foo` is
422	encoded, not opaque, so the title is recoverable) is a
423	`validate-numbering` design question, out of scope here.
424	
425	`vc-x1 fix-todo` alone only previews; `vc-x1 validate-todo`
426	is the read-only check. The `## Done` section keeps `-`
427	bullets — items aren't referenced by number once completed.
428	
429	Example shape:
430	
431	```
432	# Todo
433	- Add new feature X [details](features.md#feature-x)
434	- Fix bug Y [[1]]
435	
436	# Done
437	- Fixed issue Z [[2]],[[3]]
438	
439	[1]: bugs.md#bug-y
440	[2]: issues.md#issue-z
441	[3]: fixes.md#fix-z
442	```
443	
444	### Retiring Done entries
445	
446	`todo.md`'s `## Done` section is a rolling buffer of recently
447	shipped work, not a permanent log. Move entries into `done.md`
448	at two natural beats:
449	
450	- **Closing a ladder** — when the final `X.Y.Z` (no suffix)
451	  commit ships, decide which prior entries are no longer
452	  needed for nearby context and migrate them.
453	- **Opening a new ladder** — at `X.Y.Z-0`, do the same sweep
454	  before bumping the version-of-record.
455	
456	Migration mechanics:
457	
458	- Move the bullet itself from `todo.md > ## Done` to
459	  `done.md` (preserving the original ref number).
460	- Copy any references the moved entries cite into
461	  `done.md`'s `# References` section (those refs are
462	  file-local, so coexisting with `todo.md`'s namespace is
463	  fine).
464	- Prune any references in `todo.md > # References` no longer
465	  cited by anything in `## In Progress` / `## Todo` /
466	  `## Done`. This frees the numbers for future reuse.
467	
468	## Chores conventions
469	
470	### Headings and entries that record a commit
471	
472	A commit's title is reused verbatim across its records — see
473	[Conventional-commit shape](#conventional-commit-shape-ladder--chores--commit)
474	for the rule. Beyond the chores `##` header, that same string is
475	used for the matching `todo.md > ## Done` entry and any `[N]`
476	reference to that section. Titles carry **no `(<version>)`
477	suffix** — see
478	[Commit description](notes/cycle-protocol.md#commit-description)
479	for why.
480	E.g. the chores header `## refactor: extract config loader`
481	and the Done line `- refactor: extract config loader [[3]]`.
482	The `## Done` entry uses the close-out commit's title.
483	
484	This does **not** apply to organizational headings (`## Todo`,
485	`## In Progress`, `# References`) or to design `###` subsections
486	inside a chores section — those are named for whatever fits.
487	Among the commit-recording ones, exact match is the strong
488	default (nothing absolute): a near-miss just makes it harder to
489	line a record up with its commit.
490	
491	A commit-recording header is provisional while the work is in
492	progress; the *last* edit before `vc-x1 push` syncs it — and the
493	`## Done` entry / `[N]` anchor for that commit — to the final
494	commit title. See [Markdown anchor links](#markdown-anchor-links)
495	for the slug algorithm; the pre-commit checklist catches a
496	dangling `#anchor`, and a future `vc-x1 validate-repo` should too
497	(and should verify the recorded title matches the commit).
498	
499	Any pre-existing sections and `## Done` entries that predate
500	this convention keep their free-form text; the convention
501	applies going forward.
502	
503	### Chores section content — no edit list; git is the record
504	
505	A chores section is: a `Commits:` line (first line under the
506	header — see below), then [Prose form](#prose-form) (intro +
507	bullets) for what landed and why, and any `###` design
508	subsections. Bullets here are **conceptual** — design points,
509	structural notes — never a per-file edit list. That lives in the
510	commit message body, which is the source of truth for "what
511	changed mechanically" (immutable, `git show`-able, naturally
512	scoped to the commit). The chores section is the source of truth
513	for the design thinking; the two cross-link, neither restates the
514	other.
515	
516	When the intro starts wanting to explain a mechanism,
517	hypothesis, or wrinkle, don't inflate it — promote that to its
518	own `###` subsection inside the same `chores-NN.md`. If the
519	wrinkle is a live design concern (something that *should*
520	change, not just be recorded), also add a `notes/todo.md` item
521	with a `[N]` ref pointing at that subsection (todo→chores is the
522	normal ref direction).
523	
524	**Why:** a chores edit list and the commit body were specified
525	to be the same content in two places — and detail written twice
526	drifts. Git owns the mechanical record; chores owns the
527	narrative; `Commits:` links them.
528	
529	### Chores commit references
530	
531	The first line under a chores section header is a `Commits:`
532	line citing the git commit(s) that section records:
533	
534	```
535	## refactor: extract config loader
536	
537	Commits: [[3]]
538	
539	<intro paragraph...>
540	```
541	
542	`Commits:` uses the file-local `[N]` reference machinery (see
543	[Reference numbering](#reference-numbering)),
544	**double-bracketed** so the brackets render — `Commits: [[3]]`,
545	or `Commits: [[3]],[[5]]` for several. (`[[3]]` shows as a
546	literal `[`, the `[3]` link, then a literal `]`; the inner
547	`[3]` resolves against its `[3]:` definition — CommonMark /
548	GitHub / VS Code all do this.) The `# References` definition
549	puts the **commit URL** as the destination, with the **full
550	40-hex SHA** in the title slot:
551	
552	```
553	[3]: https://github.com/<owner>/<repo>/commit/<12-hex> "<40-hex>"
554	```
555	
556	- The 12-hex short SHA in the URL keeps it short; GitHub /
557	  GitLab resolve a unique prefix to the canonical commit page
558	  (GitLab's path has a `/-/` before `commit/`).
559	- The full SHA in the title is host-agnostic and unambiguous —
560	  it survives a repo host change, `git show <40-hex>` works in
561	  any clone, and external tooling scraping the notes (a
562	  database, say) gets the canonical identifier.
563	
564	**Timing.** A commit's SHA isn't stable until it lands on a
565	**permanent branch** (`main`, or a long-lived release/patch
566	branch) — a rebase or squash rewrites it on the way. A commit
567	can't record its own SHA, so the fill lands one push later:
568	every section opens with an **empty `Commits:`**, and each push
569	backfills the `Commits:` of the commits the previous push made
570	permanent. On a topic branch a section waits until the branch
571	lands. The commit itself is the record, and
572	`git log --grep "<title>"` finds it. See cycle-protocol
573	[Commits backfill](notes/cycle-protocol.md#commits-backfill).
574	
575	## Cycle Protocol
576	
577	Every change runs as a **cycle**: Preparation (`X.Y.Z-0`) →
578	Work commits (`X.Y.Z-1`, `X.Y.Z-2`, …) → Close-out (bare
579	`X.Y.Z`). Each commit runs through a per-commit flow whose
580	validation step is medium-specific — the Rust example is the
581	cargo cycle (fmt → clippy → test → install); skip-able for
582	notes-only commits, mandatory at close-out. The full protocol
583	— numbering, per-commit flow, reviewing changes, close-out,
584	pushing, ochid trailers, sub-cycles — lives in
585	[`notes/cycle-protocol.md`](notes/cycle-protocol.md). Read
586	it before any commit work — and before any push, cycle or
587	not: pushes always need per-push user approval (see
588	[Pushing policy](notes/cycle-protocol.md#policy)).
589	
590	**Versioning.** How this repo numbers cycles, where the
591	version-of-record lives, and its bump cadence are
592	project-specific and documented in
593	[versioning.md](notes/versioning.md) — the single source of
594	truth that AGENTS.md and cycle-protocol.md refer to
595	abstractly.
596	
597	**Hard stop after push/finalize.** Once a push or `.claude`
598	finalize is invoked (`vc-x1 push` does both), do no further
599	work — no verification, no summary, no next-step offers —
600	until the user speaks. Put all closing words *before* the
601	invoke. The harness rejects an empty turn, so it may force a
602	visible token after the tool returns; if so, emit a bare
603	acknowledgment only (e.g. "landed") — never a summary or more
604	work. Post-push verification happens next turn at the user's
605	direction. See
606	[After push or finalize](notes/cycle-protocol.md#after-push-or-finalize-stop-and-wait).
607	
608	**`vc-x1 push` behaviors to keep in mind.** Two, independent
609	of project language:
610	
611	- **Preflight** runs the cargo cycle (fmt → clippy → test). On
612	  a cargo project it works as-is, so `vc-x1 push <bookmark>`
613	  runs it before the review gate. On a project with no cargo
614	  cycle — or any time preflight can't run — invoke `vc-x1 push
615	  <bookmark> --from message ...` to skip preflight and the
616	  interactive review gate, doing the diff review in
617	  conversation first.
618	- **ochid trailers** are injected by `vc-x1 push` itself —
619	  don't hand-write them into the commit body or
620	  `--title`/`--body`.
621	
622	
623	## Code Conventions
624	
625	### Doc comments on every file, function, and method
626	
627	Every `.rs` file must begin with a `//!` module docstring. Every
628	function and method must have a `///` doc comment. Keep them brief —
629	one sentence of purpose is often enough; the discipline is that the
630	comment exists, not that it be long. Doc comments follow the
631	[Prose form](#prose-form) shape (intro + bullets).
632	
633	This is a deliberate override of the generic "write no comments"
634	default that applies to inline `//` comments. Doc comments on the
635	module / item surface are expected; inline explanatory comments
636	inside function bodies remain discouraged unless they capture a
637	non-obvious WHY.
638	
639	**Clap-derive args:** doc comments on `#[arg(...)]` fields drive
640	`--help` output. Clap reflows by default and collapses bullets
641	into running prose. Add `#[arg(verbatim_doc_comment, ...)]` on
642	any field whose doc comment uses bullets so each `- …` lands on
643	its own line in the rendered help.
644	
645	### `// OK: …` comments on `unwrap*` calls (Rust)
646	
647	Non-test code that calls `.unwrap()`, `.unwrap_or(…)`,
648	`.unwrap_or_default()`, or `.unwrap_or_else(…)` must have a trailing
649	`// OK: …` comment that justifies why the call is acceptable.
650	
651	- `// OK: <specific reason>` — document the real precondition,
652	  invariant, or domain reason. Preferred whenever the reason isn't
653	  self-evident.
654	- `// OK: obvious` — the default is self-evident from context (e.g.
655	  `desc.lines().next().unwrap_or("")` — empty desc → empty title).
656	
657	Bare `// OK` is not used (reads like a truncated comment).
658	Abbreviations (e.g. `SE`) are not used because they require a decoder
659	ring for readers seeing the code out of context.
660	
661	For provably-unreachable `.unwrap()` calls, also prefix with
662	`#[allow(clippy::unwrap_used)]` so the site stays silent if we enable
663	the project-wide `clippy::unwrap_used` lint later.
664	
665	```rust
666	let max = stderr_level.unwrap_or(LevelFilter::Info); // OK: default verbosity when -v/-vv absent
667	let first_line = desc.lines().next().unwrap_or("");  // OK: obvious
668	
669	match matches.len() {
670	    1 => {
671	        #[allow(clippy::unwrap_used)]
672	        // OK: `1 =>` arm guarantees matches.len() == 1
673	        Ok(TitleMatch::One(matches.into_iter().next().unwrap()))
674	    }
675	    // ...
676	}
677	```
678	
679	Tests (`#[cfg(test)]`) are exempt — panicking on setup failure is the
680	correct test behavior.
681	
682	### Ask for clarification on ambiguous input
683	
684	When user input is ambiguous or missing necessary detail, stop and
685	ask a specific question. Do not proceed on a guess and hope the
686	result lands right — a clarifying question costs a few seconds;
687	redoing misaligned work costs much more.
688	
689	### Recognize when stuck
690	
691	If a simple task has eaten 5+ minutes of thinking or back-and-forth
692	without progress, stop. Summarize what's blocking — unclear
693	requirements, unfamiliar API surface, conflicting signals — and ask.
694	Continued flailing produces worse outcomes than a direct "I'm stuck
695	on X."
696	
```
